Critical

Researcher Says AI Helped Develop Linux Traffic-Control Race Into Root Exploit

The Hacker News
2026-07-28 9 use cases 3 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

STAR Labs has published a Linux kernel exploit that turns an ordinary local user into root on the CentOS Stream 9 build it targeted. The flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-53264 (CVSS score: 7.8), is a use-after-free race in the kernel's network traffic-control subsystem.Researcher Lee Jia Jie said artificial intelligence (AI) helped him find the bug and speed up exploit development. Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → [LLM] CVE-2026-53264 unpatched kernel exposure (traffic-control UAF LPE) · Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Linux root process executed from memfd (CVE-2026-53264 core-dump payload) · [LLM] Unprivileged Linux tc clsact/flower/gact traffic-control manipulation (CVE-2026-53264 trigger) · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Linux core_pattern overwritten to memfd/pipe handler (CVE-2026-53264 privesc payoff)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Linux core_pattern overwritten to memfd/pipe handler (CVE-2026-53264 privesc payoff) AIInstallation High
Hunts modification of /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern by a non-standard process — the final privilege-escalation step in the STAR Labs CVE-2026-53264 traffic-control UAF exploit, which overwrites core_pattern so a crashed child runs a memfd-backed binary as the root core-dump handler. core_pattern is a global setting normally touched only by systemd/sysctl at boot, so any other writer is high-signal. Rationale: Anchored on the exact procfs path (/proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern) the article states the ROP chain overwrites; core_pattern is global and normally only written by systemd/sysctl, so a non-standard writer is near-zero-FP. Cross-checked NVD CVE-2026-53264 and Aqua Tracee's core_pattern_modification signature. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-53264 • https://aquasecurity.github.io/tracee/v0.20/docs/events/builtin/signatures/core_pattern_modification/ • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-RHEL6-KERNELDOC-17568795
ATT&CKT1068 T1546
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • systemd-sysctl applying kernel.core_pattern from /etc/sysctl.d at boot
  • Admin manually running 'sysctl -w kernel.core_pattern=...' to configure coredumpctl/abrt
  • apport (Ubuntu) or abrt (RHEL) install hooks setting their pipe handler
  • Container runtime / kdump initialization writing core_pattern
  • Ansible/Chef/Puppet hardening playbooks enforcing a core_pattern baseline
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("systemd-sysctl","sysctl","systemd","apport","abrt-install-ccpp-hook","kdump","tuned","cloud-init")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Read the current core_pattern value on the host and the InitiatingProcess/ContextImage that wrote it — a value beginning with '|' pointing to a memfd, /tmp, or a deleted binary (not /usr/lib/systemd/systemd-coredump or apport) is malicious. Check the writer's UID and whether it was launched from a user namespace. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the host, capture the running process table and any process whose executable path is memfd/(deleted), and reset core_pattern to the distro default. 3) HUNT: Look on the same host for new user+network namespace creation, tc clsact/flower activity, and a root process executed from memfd within seconds of a child crash (UC 'Linux root fileless execution from memfd').
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FolderPath has "/proc/sys/kernel" and FileName == "core_pattern"
| where ActionType in ("FileModified","FileCreated")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("systemd-sysctl","sysctl","systemd","apport","abrt-install-ccpp-hook","kdump","tuned","cloud-init")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, ActionType
| order by Timestamp desc
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where Facility in ("audit","authpriv","kern","user") or ProcessName in ("auditd","audispd")
| where SyslogMessage has "core_pattern"
| where SyslogMessage has_any ("name=\"/proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern\"","/proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern")
| where SyslogMessage !has "systemd-sysctl"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName, HostIP, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Linux core_pattern Modified to Non-Standard Handler
id: 3f1c9a52-7b84-4e2d-9c11-2a6f0d8e4b71
description: Detects modification of /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern, the final privesc step of the CVE-2026-53264 traffic-control UAF exploit which points core_pattern at a memfd-backed root core-dump handler.
references:
  - https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/researcher-says-ai-helped-develop-linux.html
  - https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-53264
  - https://aquasecurity.github.io/tracee/v0.20/docs/events/builtin/signatures/core_pattern_modification/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/28
tags:
  - attack.t1068
  - attack.t1546
logsource:
  product: linux
  category: file_event
detection:
  selection:
    TargetFilename: '/proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern'
  filter:
    Image|endswith:
      - '/systemd-sysctl'
      - '/sysctl'
      - '/systemd'
  condition: selection and not filter
level: high
source:linux.auditd (@auditd.type:PATH OR @auditd.type:SYSCALL) core_pattern -@process.name:systemd-sysctl -@process.name:sysctl
#event_simpleName=FileWritten TargetFileName=/\/proc\/sys\/kernel\/core_pattern$/i
| NOT (ContextImageFileName=/(systemd-sysctl|sysctl|systemd|cloud-init)$/i)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, TargetFileName], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where Filesystem.file_path="/proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern" (Filesystem.action=modified OR Filesystem.action=created OR Filesystem.action=write) by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.file_path Filesystem.process_id Filesystem.user | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | where NOT match(process_id, "^0$") | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where Filesystem.file_path="/proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern" (Filesystem.action=modified OR Filesystem.action=created OR Filesystem.action=write) by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.file_path Filesystem.process_id Filesystem.user | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | where NOT match(process_id, "^0$") | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
Linux root process executed from memfd (CVE-2026-53264 core-dump payload) AIExploitation High
Hunts a process running as root (UID 0) whose executable image is an anonymous memfd file. The CVE-2026-53264 exploit places a copy of itself in a memfd and deliberately crashes a child so the kernel runs the memfd-backed binary as the root core-dump handler in the init namespace — a fileless root execution with no on-disk binary. Rationale: Directly models the article's stated payoff — 'places a copy of itself in a memfd ... run the memfd-backed binary as the root core-dump handler.' A root-owned process whose image path is memfd is not produced by any legitimate service, giving very high fidelity. Corroborated against NVD CVE-2026-53264. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-53264 • https://cyberpress.org/ai-helps-discover-linux-kernel-zero-day/
ATT&CKT1620 T1068
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legit software using memfd_create + fexecve for sandboxed/anti-tamper loaders (some AV, DRM, or JIT runtimes)
  • Container tooling (e.g. runc/gVisor) that fexecs a memfd-sealed helper as root
  • Security research / red-team tooling on the host running fileless loaders
  • Package managers building in-memory transient binaries during a root install
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("runc","containerd-shim","dockerd")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the process ran as UID 0 and its image is memfd/(deleted) with no backing inode; capture the memfd contents (/proc/<pid>/exe) and hash it, and check the parent — kernel/core-dump context is the exploit tell. 2) CONTAIN: Kill the process, isolate the host, and snapshot memory before reboot to preserve the memfd payload. 3) HUNT: Correlate with a core_pattern modification and a deliberately crashed child process in the minute prior, plus new user/network namespace creation by the same unprivileged UID.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FolderPath has "memfd:" or FolderPath startswith "/memfd" or FileName startswith "memfd:"
| where AccountName == "root" or ProcessTokenElevation == "TokenElevationTypeFull"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where ProcessName in ("auditd","audispd") or Facility == "audit"
| where SyslogMessage has "EXECVE" or SyslogMessage has "exe="
| where SyslogMessage has "memfd:"
| where SyslogMessage has "uid=0" or SyslogMessage has "euid=0"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName, HostIP, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Unprivileged Linux tc clsact/flower/gact traffic-control manipulation (CVE-2026-53264 trigger) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts a non-root user driving the traffic-control path the CVE-2026-53264 UAF requires — a clsact qdisc plus a flower classifier and gact action (CONFIG_NET_CLS_FLOWER / CONFIG_NET_ACT_GACT). Surfaces the exploit's setup stage where an unprivileged process gains namespace-local CAP_NET_ADMIN and races RTM_NEWTFILTER/RTM_DELTFILTER. Hunting tier: a compiled exploit uses raw netlink and will not spawn the tc binary, but interactive tc use of clsact+flower by a normal user is itself anomalous. Rationale: Uses the exact kernel feature names the article and NVD cite (clsact qdisc, CONFIG_NET_CLS_FLOWER 'flower' classifier, CONFIG_NET_ACT_GACT 'gact' action) on the tc binary by a non-root user. Marked hunting because the PoC uses raw netlink (no tc process) — this catches interactive/adapted variants, not the compiled exploit. Corroborated by NVD/Snyk on the tcf_idr_check_alloc UAF path. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-53264 • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-RHEL6-KERNELDOC-17568795
ATT&CKT1068 T1611
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Kubernetes CNI plugins (Cilium, Calico) programming clsact/flower TC filters — often run as root though
  • Network engineers testing QoS/traffic shaping with tc in a lab
  • eBPF/XDP tooling that attaches clsact qdiscs
  • Container network setup scripts invoking tc during pod bring-up
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("cilium-agent","calico-node","kubelet","containerd")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the invoking UID is unprivileged and whether the tc call occurred inside a freshly created user/network namespace (check /proc/<pid>/ns); legitimate TC programming is usually root or a CNI service account. 2) CONTAIN: If unexplained, isolate the host and snapshot the process tree — the qdisc setup is the pre-race stage, so root compromise may be seconds away. 3) HUNT: Pivot on the same host/UID for core_pattern modification and a root memfd execution; check kernel version against the CVE-2026-53264 fixed releases.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName == "tc"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("clsact","flower","gact")
| where AccountName !in~ ("root","system") and AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where ProcessName in ("auditd","audispd") or Facility == "audit"
| where SyslogMessage has "EXECVE"
| where SyslogMessage has "/tc" and (SyslogMessage has "clsact" or SyslogMessage has "flower" or SyslogMessage has "gact")
| where SyslogMessage !has "uid=0"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName, HostIP, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Unprivileged tc clsact/flower Traffic-Control Manipulation
id: c0a4e916-2f7b-4d38-8e51-6b9d3a1c7f42
description: Detects the tc binary configuring a clsact qdisc with a flower classifier and gact action by a non-root user, matching the setup path required by the CVE-2026-53264 net/sched UAF.
references:
  - https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/researcher-says-ai-helped-develop-linux.html
  - https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-53264
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/28
tags:
  - attack.t1068
  - attack.t1611
logsource:
  product: linux
  category: process_creation
detection:
  selection:
    Image|endswith: '/tc'
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'clsact'
      - 'flower'
      - 'gact'
  condition: selection
level: medium
source:linux.auditd @auditd.type:EXECVE @process.name:tc (@process.command_line:*clsact* OR @process.command_line:*flower* OR @process.command_line:*gact*) -@user.id:0
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\/tc$/i CommandLine=/(clsact|flower|gact)/i
| NOT (UserName=/^root$/i)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT

| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.process_name="tc" (Processes.process="*clsact*" OR Processes.process="*flower*" OR Processes.process="*gact*") Processes.user!="root" by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name Processes.process Processes.parent_process_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
CVE-2026-53264 unpatched kernel exposure (traffic-control UAF LPE) AIReconnaissance High
Surfaces onboarded Linux hosts still carrying a kernel vulnerable to CVE-2026-53264. With full exploit source public and per-kernel ROP offsets, exposure prioritisation matters: fixed releases are 5.10.259, 5.15.210, 6.1.176, 6.6.143, 6.12.94, 6.18.36, 7.0.13. Pairs with the behavioural UCs to distinguish 'exposed' from 'exploited'. Rationale: Uses the concrete CVE id and the exact fixed kernel releases the article enumerates; a straight TVM/vuln-DM lookup on CVE-2026-53264 gives a clean, no-FP exposure list. Cross-checked NVD and Snyk records for the same CVE id. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-53264 • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-RHEL6-KERNELDOC-17568795
ATT&CKT1068
Data sourcesVulnerabilities.Vulnerabilities
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Scanner reports the CVE on a kernel already patched via distro backport but with an unchanged upstream version string (Debian/Ubuntu backports) — verify the distro package build, not the mainline number
  • Hosts lacking unprivileged user namespaces or CONFIG_NET_ACT_GACT/CONFIG_NET_CLS_FLOWER are exposed-but-not-exploitable
  • Stale TVM inventory after a kernel update but before reboot
  • Duplicate device entries inflating the exposed count
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: For each flagged host confirm the running kernel vs the distro-fixed build (not upstream number), and check whether unprivileged user namespaces and the CONFIG_NET_ACT_GACT/CONFIG_NET_CLS_FLOWER options are enabled — that narrows truly exploitable hosts. 2) CONTAIN: For exploitable hosts, apply the distro kernel update and reboot; as interim mitigation set kernel.unprivileged_userns_clone=0 where the workload allows. 3) HUNT: On any exposed host, run the core_pattern-modification and memfd-root-execution UCs to check for prior exploitation attempts.

| where CveId == "CVE-2026-53264"
| join kind=leftouter (DeviceInfo | summarize arg_max(Timestamp, OSDistribution, OSVersion) by DeviceId) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, OSDistribution, OSVersion, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by DeviceName asc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc

``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/

    category: process_creation

            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'

            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'

            - 'hxxp'

            - 'wget '

        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] AppVShNotify.exe spawning child processes (NightLedger command dispatcher execution) · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Mirage Kitten targets Middle East and Africa region with new malware
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] NightLedger DLL search-order hijack: SspiCli.dll side-loaded by AppVShNotify.exe · [LLM] BridgeHead/ArcBridge tunneler deployment: unbcl.dll & libwinpthread-1.dll in anomalous paths · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] NightLedger C2 beacon to realhealthshop[.]com / tjconsultingservices[.]com with hardcoded URIs · [LLM] BridgeHead WebSocket SOCKS5 tunnel to smartconnect.azurewebsites.net with hardcoded Edg/86 UA · [LLM] Mirage Kitten (UNC1549) infrastructure & payload IOC sweep · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
NightLedger C2 beacon to realhealthshop[.]com / tjconsultingservices[.]com with hardcoded URIs AICommand & Control High
Hunts Mirage Kitten's NightLedger backdoor beaconing over HTTPS to its primary C2 realhealthshop[.]com (fallback tjconsultingservices[.]com) using the fixed, high-entropy URI paths /edfcvfgbhnjmkqwasderfgg (GET beacon), /qasxcdfvgbhnmyuioplkhnj (file upload POST) and /wsdefvvbnhyuijkplmbgfrtt (command-output POST). Article-specific because these random-looking endpoints and domains do not collide with legitimate traffic. Rationale: Anchored on the exact C2 domains and three fixed high-entropy URI endpoints from the article body; corroborated by The Hacker News and TechNadu write-ups of the same Securelist report. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/nimbus-manticore-deploys-nightledger.html • https://www.technadu.com/mirage-kittens-new-malware-toolkit-targets-aerospace-and-defense-across-middle-east-and-africa/632036/ • https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/actor/unc1549
ATT&CKT1071.001
Data sourcesWeb Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimately registered marketing/health domain sharing the realhealthshop string (verify full FQDN)
  • Proxy logs replaying cached traffic during a DFIR export skewing timestamps
  • Security researcher/sandbox detonating the sample inside the corporate proxy
  • Threat-intel feed connector actively resolving the IOC domain for enrichment
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe")  // real NightLedger initiates from AppVShNotify.exe, not a browser
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the InitiatingProcessFileName on the beaconing host — NightLedger runs inside AppVShNotify.exe via SspiCli.dll side-load; confirm parent/module. 2) Contain: isolate the host, block realhealthshop[.]com and tjconsultingservices[.]com at proxy/DNS/firewall, capture memory. 3) Hunt: pivot on the two POST URIs to spot exfil, search other hosts for AppVShNotify.exe loading SspiCli.dll from non-System32 paths and the mutex A8215357-F99A-44FE-BC65-D8F0434B0C03.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("realhealthshop.com","tjconsultingservices.com")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DestinationHostName has_any ("realhealthshop.com","tjconsultingservices.com")
    or RequestURL has_any ("/edfcvfgbhnjmkqwasderfgg","/qasxcdfvgbhnmyuioplkhnj","/wsdefvvbnhyuijkplmbgfrtt")
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceName=SourceHostName, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, RequestURL, RequestMethod, RequestClientApplication
| order by TimeGenerated desc

NightLedger DLL search-order hijack: SspiCli.dll side-loaded by AppVShNotify.exe AIInstallation High
Detects NightLedger's masquerade as SspiCli.dll abusing DLL search-order hijacking against the legitimate AppVShNotify.exe (which delay-loads SspiCli.dll via RPCRT4.dll on authenticated RPC calls). Fires when SspiCli.dll is loaded/written from a path outside System32/SysWOW64/WinSxS, i.e. co-located with AppVShNotify.exe. Rationale: Uses the article's explicit hijack mechanism (SspiCli.dll masquerade loaded by legitimate AppVShNotify.exe via RPCRT4.dll delay-load) with a System32/WinSxS path exclusion so only the co-located malicious copy fires. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/mirage-kitten-new-tools/120811/ • https://www.nextron-systems.com/2026/06/01/detecting-nimbus-manticore-and-their-sideloading-infection-chains/
ATT&CKT1574.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Microsoft App-V Client legitimately installed in a non-standard drive/folder still loading the genuine System32 SspiCli.dll (path check mitigates)
  • Third-party installer bundling a private SspiCli.dll copy for compatibility
  • EDR sensor reporting the load with a normalized short path that trips the exclusion
  • App-V redirected/virtualized package overlay showing a non-system module path
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith @"C:\Program Files\Microsoft Application Virtualization\Client" or SHA256 != ""  // keep only anomalous co-located copies; validate hash against signed baseline
Response runbook
1) Triage: hash the loaded SspiCli.dll (compare SHA256 to Microsoft's signed copy) and confirm the FolderPath equals AppVShNotify.exe's directory. 2) Contain: isolate host, quarantine the rogue DLL, kill the AppVShNotify.exe process tree. 3) Hunt: sweep the fleet for the same DLL name/path and for outbound HTTPS to realhealthshop[.]com/tjconsultingservices[.]com from AppVShNotify.exe.

| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "sspicli.dll"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "appvshnotify.exe"
| where FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\System32" and FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\SysWOW64" and FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\WinSxS"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

AppVShNotify.exe spawning child processes (NightLedger command dispatcher execution) AIExploitation Medium
Detects NightLedger command execution and discovery: because the backdoor runs inside a hijacked AppVShNotify.exe, any child process it spawns (command 3 'execute a process', plus recon like tasklist/whoami/net) is highly anomalous — AppVShNotify.exe is an App-V notifier that essentially never launches child processes. Rationale: Article states NightLedger executes processes (command 3), enumerates processes (command 90) and collects NetSetup.log while running inside the hijacked AppVShNotify.exe; that parent virtually never spawns children, making the parent pivot high-fidelity. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/mirage-kitten-new-tools/120811/
ATT&CKT1059 T1057 T1083
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Genuine App-V virtualized application legitimately invoking a child helper during package publish/refresh
  • Microsoft App-V troubleshooting/repair spawning conhost.exe
  • SCCM/Intune App-V sequencing operations during deployment
  • Security tooling injecting into AppVShNotify.exe for instrumentation
Suppression filter
| where ChildProcess !in~ ("conhost.exe","werfault.exe")  // strip benign App-V helper spawns; keep interpreters/recon binaries
Response runbook
1) Triage: examine child ChildCmd for recon (tasklist, whoami, net, ipconfig) or reads of C:\Windows\debug\NetSetup.log; confirm AppVShNotify.exe module list contains rogue SspiCli.dll. 2) Contain: isolate host, kill the AppVShNotify.exe tree, quarantine the side-loaded DLL. 3) Hunt: correlate with beacons to realhealthshop[.]com and look for BridgeHead DLLs (unbcl.dll / libwinpthread-1.dll) fleet-wide.

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"

         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]

        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac") or SHA1 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac") or MD5 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac") or SHA1 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac") or MD5 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    where Filesystem.file_hash IN ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.file_name, Filesystem.file_hash
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`

Critical

Attackers Exploit Arista VeloCloud Orchestrator Command Injection Flaw

The Hacker News
2026-07-28 9 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

A maximum-severity security flaw impacting on-premises versions of Arista VeloCloud Orchestrator (VCO) has come under active exploitation in the wild. The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-16812 (CVSS score: 10.0), is a case of operating system command injection that could pave the way for arbitrary code execution. "VeloCloud Orchestrator (VCO) on-prem has a security issue where this issue

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Inbound access to Arista VeloCloud Orchestrator web interface from CVE-2026-16812 attacker IPs · [LLM] OS command-injection payload patterns in VeloCloud Orchestrator web access logs · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Outbound C2/exfil from internal host to VeloCloud CVE-2026-16812 attacker IPs · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Inbound access to Arista VeloCloud Orchestrator web interface from CVE-2026-16812 attacker IPs AIExploitation Medium
Hunts inbound network/web traffic sourced from the three Arista-attributed attacker IPs (8.19.75.217, 206.72.242.124, 206.72.242.162) reaching an internet-facing VeloCloud Orchestrator (VCO) web interface. These IPs were named by Arista Security Advisory 0144 as responsible for actively exploiting the unauthenticated OS command-injection flaw, so any appearance as a source of web/management traffic is high-fidelity. Rationale: Anchored on the three IPs Arista Security Advisory 0144 explicitly names as conducting the CVE-2026-16812 attacks (corroborated by CISA KEV and BleepingComputer); Arista directly instructs operators to block these IPs and review logs for their presence. Attacker-attributed IOC IPs are near-zero-FP. Cross-checked against: • https://www.arista.com/en/support/advisories-notices/security-advisory/24364-security-advisory-0144 • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/arista-patches-velocloud-orchestrator-zero-day-exploited-in-attacks/ • https://www.securityweek.com/critical-arista-velocloud-orchestrator-vulnerability-exploited-as-zero-day/
ATT&CKT1190 T1133
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A threat-intel or attack-surface scanning vendor you subscribe to reusing one of these IPs after takedown/reassignment
  • Your own external penetration test or ASM scan happening to egress from a cloud IP later attributed elsewhere
  • Shared-hosting/CDN IP reallocation months after the campaign
  • Firewall NAT logging that records the IP but the connection was already dropped/denied
Suppression filter
| where DeviceAction != "deny" and DeviceAction != "blocked"
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm whether the destination of the inbound flow is an internet-exposed VeloCloud Orchestrator and whether DeviceAction/action shows allowed vs blocked; if allowed, pull the matching VCO web-access log lines for the same timestamp. 2) CONTAIN: Block all three IPs at the perimeter, restrict the VCO web interface to trusted admin networks, and if the request was allowed, isolate the VCO host and begin evidence preservation (web-access, backend app, system, and DB logs) before patching to the fixed release (5.2.3.14 / 6.1.3.4 / 6.4.2.4 / 7.0.0.1). 3) HUNT: Pivot to UC #2 (injection payloads in the web logs) and UC #3 (outbound callbacks) for the same host and rotate VCO/Edge credentials, certificates and keys per Arista guidance.

OS command-injection payload patterns in VeloCloud Orchestrator web access logs AIExploitation Medium
Hunts VeloCloud Orchestrator web/proxy access logs for HTTP requests whose URL or query string carries shell metacharacters, command-substitution syntax, or encoded equivalents — the exploitation shape of the unauthenticated OS command-injection flaw CVE-2026-16812. Arista's own indicator list calls out web requests containing encoded characters and references to internal services, making the request content the pivot regardless of source IP. Rationale: Uses the request-content indicators Arista published for CVE-2026-16812 (encoded characters / internal-service references in web requests) combined with the vulnerability class (OS command injection). Hunting-tier and source-agnostic because the article/advisory publish no endpoint path or parameter name — the metacharacter set is the only groundable signal, so it needs analyst triage. Cross-checked against: • https://www.arista.com/en/support/advisories-notices/security-advisory/24364-security-advisory-0144 • https://www.rescana.com/post/active-exploitation-alert-arista-velocloud-orchestrator-cve-2026-16812-command-injection-vulnerability-enables-remote-co • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/arista-patches-velocloud-orchestrator-zero-day-exploited-in-attacks/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.004
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate VCO admin/API calls that URL-encode pipe or semicolon characters inside filter or query parameters
  • SSO/OAuth redirect URLs carrying encoded '&' and ';' in state parameters
  • Monitoring/health-check probes that append query strings
  • Automation or config-push tooling that legitimately passes list parameters delimited by ';' or '|'
  • Security scanners you own probing the same interface
Suppression filter
| where RequestMethod in ("POST","GET") and RequestURL matches regex @"(?i)(%24%28|\$\(|%60|`|\$\{IFS|%3b[a-z/]|;[a-z/])"
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Decode the flagged RequestURL/uri_query and judge whether the metacharacters form an executable command (e.g. `;id`, `$(...)`, backticked subshell, `${IFS}`) versus benign encoded delimiters; correlate SourceIP against the UC #1 attacker-IP list. 2) CONTAIN: If a plausible injection is confirmed on an internet-facing VCO, restrict the web interface to trusted admin nets, snapshot and preserve web/app/system/DB logs, and patch to the fixed VCO release. 3) HUNT: On the same host look for spawned shells, file/archive creation, database exports and outbound callbacks (UC #3), then rotate VCO and downstream Edge credentials, certs and keys.
let injTokens = dynamic([";","|","`","$(","&&","%3b","%7c","%60","%24%28","%26%26","${IFS","$%7bIFS"]);
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where isnotempty(RequestURL)
| where RequestURL has_any (injTokens)
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct, SourceIP, DestinationIP, DestinationPort, RequestMethod, RequestURL, RequestClientApplication, DeviceAction
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Outbound C2/exfil from internal host to VeloCloud CVE-2026-16812 attacker IPs AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts outbound HTTP/HTTPS connections from any internal host — especially a VeloCloud Orchestrator — to the three Arista-attributed attacker IPs, sizing transferred bytes to catch beaconing or data staging. Arista specifically warns to check for unexpected outbound network activity from the VCO host after exploitation; an outbound flow to attacker infrastructure indicates established post-exploitation control rather than a blocked inbound probe. Rationale: Combines the attacker-attributed IPs (Arista SA 0144 / CISA KEV) with Arista's explicit guidance to hunt unexpected outbound HTTP/HTTPS from the VCO host, scoped to web ports and aggregated on bytes_out to surface beaconing/exfil. Outbound to named C2 infra is a distinct, high-fidelity pivot from the inbound exploitation in UC #1. Cross-checked against: • https://www.arista.com/en/support/advisories-notices/security-advisory/24364-security-advisory-0144 • https://securityaffairs.com/196130/security/u-s-cisa-adds-arista-velocloud-orchestrator-and-fortinet-fortios-flaws-to-its-known-exploited-vulnerabilities-catalog.html • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/arista-patches-velocloud-orchestrator-zero-day-exploited-in-attacks/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1041
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A monitoring/threat-intel appliance you operate that actively probes or sinkholes these IPs
  • IP reassignment after campaign takedown so a benign service now answers on the same address
  • Reverse-DNS/geolocation enrichment tools resolving the IP as part of triage automation
  • A honeypot or deception host you run that is expected to touch attacker infra
Suppression filter
| where not (DeviceName has_any ("honeypot","decoy","sinkhole") or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("nessus.exe","nmap.exe"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Identify the internal source host and the initiating process; if the source is the VeloCloud Orchestrator or another internet-facing appliance, treat as likely post-exploitation and check byte volume for exfil sizing. 2) CONTAIN: Block the three IPs egress-wide, isolate the source host, and preserve VCO web/app/system/DB logs and file-system timestamps before rebuilding from a trusted image per Arista guidance. 3) HUNT: Correlate back to UC #1/#2 for the initial inbound exploitation window, review recent VCO admin activity and config changes, validate managed Edge device state, and rotate all VCO/Edge credentials, certificates and cryptographic keys.

Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-16812", "CVE-2025-68686", "CVE-2026-16723")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-16812", "CVE-2025-68686", "CVE-2026-16723")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Office Application Spawning Script Host
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d07
status: experimental
description: |
    Word/Excel/Outlook/OneNote spawning powershell, cmd, wscript,
    cscript, mshta, rundll32, regsvr32 — classic macro / embedded-
    object payload signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059
    - attack.t1566.001
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\winword.exe'
            - '\excel.exe'
            - '\powerpnt.exe'
            - '\outlook.exe'
            - '\onenote.exe'
            - '\mspub.exe'
            - '\visio.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child
falsepositives:
    - Legitimate automation macros in trusted internal documents
level: high
ENDPOINT

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) Exploitation High
Browser-pasted PowerShell launched from explorer/Run dialog — ClickFix social engineering.
ATT&CKT1204.004 T1059.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\explorer.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\RuntimeBroker.exe"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)

ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
